## GHSA-wrhw-j3f9-8vc6 · mcp-atlassian

2026-09-22 · Python · **CRITICAL** 10.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→SQL`

### Root cause

The application was vulnerable to JQL injection because it did not properly sanitize user-supplied project filters before incorporating them into JQL queries. Additionally, it lacked robust SSRF protection, allowing for potential server-side request forgery through redirect validation and DNS rebinding attacks.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass configured project restrictions in Jira, potentially accessing or manipulating data outside their authorized scope. The SSRF vulnerabilities could allow an attacker to make arbitrary requests from the server, potentially accessing internal network resources or sensitive cloud metadata.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp_atlassian/jira/search.py
+++ b/src/mcp_atlassian/jira/search.py
-            filter_to_use = projects_filter or self.config.projects_filter
-            if filter_to_use:
-                projects = [p.strip() for p in filter_to_use.split(",")]
+            for filter_str in (self.config.projects_filter, projects_filter):
+                if filter_str:
+                    jql = self._and_projects_filter(jql, filter_str)
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors JQL filtering to ensure that the configured project allowlist is always ANDed with any user-supplied filters, preventing bypass. It also introduces SSRF protection by validating redirects and pinning DNS resolution to prevent rebinding attacks.

## GHSA-3r68-hf9h-887v · mcp-atlassian

2026-09-22 · Python · **HIGH** 8.8 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The application allowed an attacker to bypass configured project filters in Jira by crafting specific JQL queries, potentially accessing unauthorized project data. Additionally, it lacked robust SSRF protections, making it vulnerable to DNS rebinding attacks and unvalidated redirects.

### Impact

An attacker could access sensitive Jira project data they are not authorized to view. They could also potentially force the application to make requests to internal network resources or bypass other network restrictions via SSRF.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp_atlassian/jira/search.py
+++ b/src/mcp_atlassian/jira/search.py
@@ -43,7 +94,9 @@ def search_issues(
                   start from the first page.
             limit: Maximum issues to return
             expand: Optional items to expand (comma-separated)
-            projects_filter: Optional comma-separated list of project keys to filter by, overrides config
+            projects_filter: Optional comma-separated list of project keys to narrow
+                results within the configured allowlist (ANDed with config, never
+                replaces it)
             page_token: Optional pagination token from a previous search result.
                   Cloud only — Server/DC uses start for pagination.
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `_apply_projects_filter` method to consistently and securely enforce project filtering on all JQL queries, ensuring that user-supplied filters can only narrow, not override, the configured allowlist. It also adds SSRF protections by validating redirects and pinning DNS resolutions to prevent rebinding attacks.

## GHSA-m3c6-2p7h-cfr3 · github.com/kubeedge/kubeedge

2026-09-22 · Go · **HIGH** 8.8 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→COMMAND`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the `keadm config-update` command was constructed by directly concatenating user-controlled input from `spec.UpdateFields` into a single string, which was then executed via `execs.NewCommand`. This allowed an attacker to inject arbitrary shell commands by crafting malicious values in the `updateKey` or `updateVal` fields.

### Impact

An attacker could achieve remote code execution on the edge nodes where KubeEdge is running, potentially leading to full compromise of the affected systems.

### Key diff

```
--- a/edge/pkg/taskmanager/actions/configupdatejob.go
+++ b/edge/pkg/taskmanager/actions/configupdatejob.go
@@ -98,15 +100,11 @@ func (h *configUpdateJobActionHandler) updateConfig(
 		return resp
 	}
 
-	var setFields string
-	for updateKey, updateVal := range spec.UpdateFields {
-		setFields = setFields + fmt.Sprintf("%s=%s,", updateKey, updateVal)
-	}
-	setFields = strings.TrimSuffix(setFields, ",")
-	cmdStr := execs.NewCommand(fmt.Sprintf("keadm config-update --set %s", setFields))
-	err := cmdStr.Exec()
+	args := buildConfigUpdateArgs(spec.UpdateFields)
+	cmd := exec.Command("keadm", args...)
+	out, err := cmd.CombinedOutput()
 	if err != nil {
-		resp.err = err
+		resp.err = fmt.Errorf("update config failed, err: %w, output: %s", err, out)
 		return resp
 	}
 	return resp
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors the command execution by using `os/exec.Command` with separate arguments instead of a single formatted string. A new `buildConfigUpdateArgs` function now properly separates the command and its arguments, preventing shell injection by ensuring that user-controlled values are passed as distinct arguments to the `keadm` command.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 96 occurrences**

## GHSA-vxg7-f2jj-jmqm · github.com/projectdiscovery/nuclei/v3

2026-09-22 · Go · **HIGH** 8.6 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from Nuclei using a forked version of the Goja JavaScript engine (github.com/Mzack9999/goja). This forked version likely contained a critical vulnerability that allowed for arbitrary code execution when processing untrusted JavaScript code within Nuclei templates.

### Impact

An attacker could craft malicious Nuclei templates containing JavaScript code that, when executed by Nuclei, would lead to arbitrary code execution on the system running Nuclei, potentially compromising the host.

### Key diff

```
--- github.com/Mzack9999/goja
+++ github.com/projectdiscovery/goja
```

### Fix summary

The patch updates all imports of the Goja JavaScript engine and its related modules from the vulnerable 'github.com/Mzack9999/goja' fork to the maintained and secured 'github.com/projectdiscovery/goja' version. This change ensures that Nuclei utilizes a patched and secure JavaScript engine.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 690 occurrences**

---

## GHSA-328g-jx67-v94g · github.com/tinyauthapp/tinyauth

2026-09-22 · Go · **HIGH** 8.1 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTHZ→RESOURCE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a case-sensitive comparison of application ACLs against hostnames, while the hostnames themselves were treated case-insensitively by the underlying system. This mismatch allowed an attacker to bypass access controls by using a different casing for the hostname than what was specified in the ACL.

### Impact

An authenticated user could gain unauthorized access to applications they were not explicitly allowed to reach, potentially leading to data exposure or unauthorized actions within those applications.

### Key diff

```
--- a/internal/service/kubernetes_service.go
+++ b/internal/service/kubernetes_service.go
@@ -235,10 +235,12 @@ func (k *KubernetesService) updateFromItem(item *unstructured.Unstructured) {
 
 		if !registerApp {
 			for _, host := range hosts {
-				if strings.HasPrefix(strings.ToLower(host), strings.ToLower(name+".")) {
+				if strings.HasPrefix(strings.ToLower(host), strings.ToLower(name+".")) { // This line was added
 					registerApp = true
 					break
 				}
 			}
 		}
```

### Fix summary

The patch refactors how ingress entries are stored and matched, removing the `domainIndex` and `appNameIndex` which were prone to the case-sensitivity issue. It introduces a `validators.NewDomainValidator` to ensure hostnames are handled consistently and explicitly checks for case-insensitive matches using `strings.ToLower` when comparing app names with ingress hosts.

## GHSA-92cr-jxw4-5wjg · @sync-in/server

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 8.1 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The application did not properly enforce 2FA for the /api/auth/token endpoint. An attacker could obtain a valid access token after initial authentication without providing the second factor, effectively bypassing the 2FA mechanism.

### Impact

An attacker could gain unauthorized access to a user's account, even if 2FA was enabled, by simply providing valid login credentials and then requesting a token without the 2FA code.

### Key diff

```
--- a/backend/src/authentication/auth.controller.spec.ts
+++ b/backend/src/authentication/auth.controller.spec.ts
@@ -14,15 +21,24 @@ import { DB_TOKEN_PROVIDER } from '../infrastructure/database/constants'
 import { AuthConfig } from './auth.config'
 import { AuthController } from './auth.controller'
 import { AuthManager } from './auth.service'
-import { TOKEN_PATHS } from './constants/auth'
-import { LoginResponseDto } from './dto/login-response.dto'
+import { ACCESS_KEY, CSRF_KEY, TOKEN_PATHS, TWO_FA_HEADER_CODE, TWO_FA_HEADER_PASSWORD } from './constants/auth'
+import { API_AUTH_SETTINGS, API_AUTH_TOKEN, API_TWO_FA_ADMIN_RESET_USER, API_TWO_FA_ENABLE, API_TWO_FA_LOGIN_VERIFY } from './constants/routes'
+import { AUTH_TOKEN_SKIP } from './decorators/auth-token-skip.decorator'
+import { LoginResponseDto, LoginVerify2FaDto } from './dto/login-response.dto'
+import { AuthLocalGuard } from './guards/auth-local.guard'
+import { JwtPayload } from './interfaces/jwt-payload.interface'
 import { TOKEN_TYPE } from './interfaces/token.interface'
 import { AuthProvider2FA } from './providers/two-fa/auth-provider-two-fa.service'
-import { AuthTwoFaGuard } from './providers/two-fa/auth-two-fa-guard'
+import { AuthTokenTwoFaGuard } from './providers/two-fa/guards/auth-token-two-fa.guard'
+import { AuthTokenTwoFaStrategy } from './providers/two-fa/guards/auth-token-two-fa.strategy'
+import { AuthTwoFaVerificationGuard } from './providers/two-fa/guards/auth-two-fa-verification.guard'
+import { TwoFaVerifyDto, TwoFaVerifyWithPasswordDto } from './providers/two-fa/auth-two-fa.dtos'
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces new 2FA-specific token types (ACCESS_2FA, CSRF_2FA) and guards (AuthTokenTwoFaGuard, AuthTwoFaVerificationGuard). It modifies the login process to set temporary 2FA cookies when 2FA is required and ensures that the /api/auth/token endpoint is protected by the new 2FA verification guards.

## GHSA-5mj8-gf6m-fhw8 · 9router

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 7.3 · Pattern: `MISSING_AUTH→ENDPOINT`

### Root cause

The vulnerability description mentions an 'Authentication Bypass in Public LLM API via Spoofable X-9r-Real-Ip Header'. However, the provided diff does not directly address an 'X-9r-Real-Ip Header' issue. Instead, the diff shows a change in how the 'CodeBuddy' OAuth provider handles token polling and refresh. Specifically, it changes the token polling from a POST request with a JSON body to a GET request with the state in the query parameter, and modifies the token refresh to send the refresh token in a custom header ('X-Refresh-Token') instead of a body. The original vulnerability likely stemmed from an incorrect or insecure implementation of the authentication flow for the CodeBuddy provider, possibly allowing an attacker to bypass authentication by manipulating request parameters or headers, though the exact mechanism related to 'X-9r-Real-Ip' is not visible in this diff.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially bypass authentication for the CodeBuddy LLM API, gaining unauthorized access to the service and its functionalities, which could include making unauthorized API calls or accessing sensitive information.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/lib/oauth/providers.js
+++ b/src/lib/oauth/providers.js
-      const response = await fetch(config.tokenUrl, {
-        method: "POST",
-        body: JSON.stringify({ state: deviceCode }),
+      const response = await fetch(`${config.tokenUrl}?state=${encodeURIComponent(deviceCode)}`, {
+        method: "GET",
--- a/open-sse/services/tokenRefresh/providers.js
+++ b/open-sse/services/tokenRefresh/providers.js
-    // No refresh token support for CodeBuddy in the original implementation
+    // CodeBuddy (Tencent) refresh — POST /v2/plugin/auth/token/refresh with the
+    // refresh token carried in the X-Refresh-Token header (not a form body),
+    // matching the official CodeBuddy CLI. Response: { code: 0, data: <token> }.
```

### Fix summary

The patch renames the 'codebuddy' provider to 'codebuddy-cn' and significantly refactors its token polling and refresh mechanisms to align with the official CodeBuddy CLI's behavior. This includes changing the token polling from a POST request with a JSON body to a GET request with the state as a query parameter, and modifying the token refresh to use a POST request with the refresh token in the 'X-Refresh-Token' header.

## GHSA-4825-p4xm-pcf2 · spree_api

2026-09-22 · Ruby · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `IDOR→DATA_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The `associate` endpoint for carts allowed an authenticated user to associate any guest cart with their account by only knowing the cart's ID. There was no check for possession of the cart's unique token, which is the actual credential authorizing control over a guest cart.

### Impact

An attacker could take over any guest user's shopping cart, potentially viewing or modifying its contents, leading to data manipulation or unauthorized purchases if the cart was later used by the legitimate guest.

### Key diff

```
--- a/spree/api/app/controllers/spree/api/v3/store/carts_controller.rb
+++ b/spree/api/app/controllers/spree/api/v3/store/carts_controller.rb
@@ -82,10 +82,15 @@ def destroy
           end
 
           # PATCH /api/v3/store/carts/:id/associate
-          # Associates a guest cart with the currently authenticated user
-          # Requires: JWT authentication + cart ID in URL
+          # Associates a guest cart with the currently authenticated user.
+          # Requires: JWT authentication + cart ID in URL + possession of the
+          # cart's token (x-spree-token). The token is the credential that
+          # authorizes claiming the cart, so it is required even when the cart
+          # is already the caller's own.
           def associate
             @cart = find_cart_for_association
+            authorize!(:update, @cart, cart_token)
+            require_cart_token!
 
             result = Spree.cart_associate_service.call(guest_order: @cart, user: current_user, guest_only: true)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces two new security checks in the `associate` action: `authorize!(:update, @cart, cart_token)` and `require_cart_token!`. The `require_cart_token!` method explicitly validates that the `x-spree-token` header matches the cart's actual token, ensuring that only the legitimate owner (or someone with the token) can associate the cart.

## GHSA-6529-c226-h328 · mcp-atlassian

2026-09-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application was missing comprehensive Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) protections, specifically for basic-auth and OAuth authentication branches, allowing an attacker to potentially redirect requests to internal resources. Additionally, the JQL project filtering logic was not robust enough, allowing a crafted JQL query to bypass project restrictions.

### Impact

An attacker could force the application to make requests to arbitrary internal or external systems, potentially accessing sensitive internal services or data. The JQL injection could allow an attacker to bypass configured project access restrictions and view or manipulate data in unauthorized Jira projects.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
+++ b/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
@@ -130,8 +132,18 @@ def __init__(self, config: ConfluenceConfig | None = None) -> None:
             client_key_password=self.config.client_key_password,
         )
 
-        # Apply opt-in HTTP hardening after SSL setup so SSLIgnoreAdapter, if
-        # mounted, also picks up the configured behavior.
+        # Validate redirects for SSRF on every outbound call from this session
+        # (covers direct _session.get() paths and global/stdio fetchers, not just
+        # the per-user HTTP path).
+        self.confluence._session.hooks["response"].append(make_ssrf_redirect_hook())
+        # Pin DNS resolution against rebinding: resolve+validate once and connect
+        # to that address, closing the validate→reconnect TOCTOU. Preserves TLS SNI.
+        mount_ssrf_pinning(self.confluence._session)
+
+        # Apply opt-in HTTP hardening after SSL setup and after the pinning
+        # adapter is mounted: these wrappers patch send() in place on whatever
+        # adapters are mounted now, so mounting the pinning adapter later would
+        # silently drop them.
         configure_retry(self.confluence._session, service="Confluence")
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces SSRF redirect validation hooks and DNS pinning to prevent rebinding attacks for all outbound HTTP requests. It also refactors the JQL project filtering to ensure that the configured project allowlist is always applied and cannot be bypassed or overridden by user-supplied JQL, even if it already contains project clauses.

## GHSA-vv3m-f8x4-7377 · lightrag-hku

2026-09-22 · Python · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application's image download guard, intended to prevent Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) by checking if resolved IP addresses are globally routable, failed to correctly classify certain IPv6 transition addresses. Specifically, the RFC 8215 local-use NAT64 prefix (64:ff9b:1::/48) was not properly handled, allowing an attacker to craft URLs that would resolve to internal network resources via this IPv6 block.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass the native-markdown image-download guard and make the server send requests to arbitrary internal network resources, potentially accessing sensitive information or interacting with internal services.

### Key diff

```
--- a/lightrag/parser/markdown/parser.py
+++ b/lightrag/parser/markdown/parser.py
@@ -249,12 +260,15 @@ def _unwrap_embedded_ipv4(ip):
     internal target (loopback / RFC1918 / cloud metadata). We therefore decode
     the embedded IPv4 and classify by *that* before the ``is_global`` gate.
 
-    Handled wrappers: IPv4-mapped (``::ffff:0:0/96``), IPv4-compatible
-    (``::/96``, minus ``::``/``::1``), the NAT64 well-known prefix
-    (``64:ff9b::/96``, RFC 6052) and the RFC 8215 local-use prefix
-    (``64:ff9b:1::/48``), and 6to4 (``2002::/16``). A NAT64-wrapped *public*
-    IPv4 (e.g. ``64:ff9b::808:808`` = 8.8.8.8) still resolves to a global IPv4
-    and is left allowed; genuine native global IPv6 is returned untouched.
+    Only wrappers whose embedded-IPv4 position is fixed by spec are decoded:
+    IPv4-mapped (``::ffff:0:0/96``), IPv4-compatible (``::/96``, minus
+    ``::``/``::1``), the NAT64 well-known prefix (``64:ff9b::/96``, RFC 6052
+    §2.2 — IPv4 in the low 32 bits) and 6to4 (``2002::/16``). A wrapper of a
+    *public* IPv4 (e.g. ``64:ff9b::808:808`` = 8.8.8.8) still resolves to a
+    global IPv4 and is left allowed; genuine native global IPv6 is returned
+    untouched. The RFC 8215 local-use prefix ``64:ff9b:1::/48`` is deliberately
+    NOT decoded here — its embedded-IPv4 position is not guaranteed, so it is
+    default-denied via :data:`_FORCE_NON_GLOBAL_V6` instead.
 
     Boundary: a NAT64 deployment using a *custom*, globally-routable Network-
     Specific Prefix (RFC 6052 permits any /32../96) is not recognised here — the
@@ -279,12 +293,23 @@ def _unwrap_embedded_ipv4(ip):
     # NAT64 well-known prefix ``64:ff9b::/96`` — IPv4 in the low 32 bits.
     if b[:12] == b"\x00\x64\xff\x9b" + b"\x00" * 8:
         return ip_address(b[12:])
-    # RFC 8215 local-use NAT64 ``64:ff9b:1::/48``.
-    if b[:6] == b"\x00\x64\xff\x9b\x00\x01":
-        return ip_address(b[12:])
     return ip
 
 
+def _is_globally_routable(ip) -> bool:
+    """True iff ``ip`` is safe to fetch (globally routable public address).
+
+    Takes the ``is_global`` verdict on the IPv4 embedded in any IPv6 transition
+    wrapper (see :func:`_unwrap_embedded_ipv4`) so a NAT64 / 6to4 /
+    IPv4-compatible literal that wraps an internal IPv4 is judged by that
+    internal IPv4. Blocks that are non-global irrespective of the interpreter's
+    classification are force-denied via :data:`_FORCE_NON_GLOBAL_V6`.
+    """
+    if ip.version == 6 and any(ip in net for net in _FORCE_NON_GLOBAL_V6):
+        return False
+    return _unwrap_embedded_ipv4(ip).is_global
+
+
 def _validated_addresses(host: str) -> list[str]:
     """Resolve ``host`` and return its addresses iff ALL are safe to fetch.
 
@@ -292,8 +317,8 @@ def _validated_addresses(host: str) -> list[str]:
     SSRF to loopback / private / link-local / reserved / CGNAT ``100.64.0.0/10``
     / TEST-NET and any other non-globally-routable range is blocked) or it
     matches the operator-configured ``NATIVE_MD_IMAGE_ALLOWED_NON_PUBLIC_CIDRS``
-    escape hatch. The ``is_global`` verdict is taken on the IPv4 embedded in any
-    IPv6 transition wrapper (see :func:`_unwrap_embedded_ipv4`) so a NAT64 /
+    escape hatch. Global-routability is decided by :func:`_is_globally_routable`,
+    which judges the IPv4 embedded in any IPv6 transition wrapper so a NAT64 /
     6to4 / IPv4-compatible literal that wraps an internal IPv4 cannot smuggle
     past the gate. Returns ``[]`` when resolution fails or *any* resolved
     address is non-public — so a single poisoned A/AAAA record rejects the whole
@@ -316,8 +341,7 @@ def _validated_addresses(host: str) -> list[str]:
             ip = ip_address(addr)
         except ValueError:
             return []
-        judged = _unwrap_embedded_ipv4(ip)
-        if not (judged.is_global or any(ip in net for net in allow)):
+        if not (_is_globally_routable(ip) or any(ip in net for net in allow)):
             return []
         addrs.append(addr)
     return addrs
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `_FORCE_NON_GLOBAL_V6` tuple to explicitly mark the RFC 8215 local-use NAT64 prefix (64:ff9b:1::/48) as non-globally-routable. It also refactors the IP classification logic into a new `_is_globally_routable` function, ensuring that this specific IPv6 block is always denied, regardless of how the embedded IPv4 might be interpreted or if the interpreter misclassifies it.

## GHSA-xj3h-wwxq-gfcj · github.com/cloudreve/Cloudreve/v4

2026-09-22 · Go · **HIGH** 7.1 · Pattern: `RACE_CONDITION→TOCTOU`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from a Time-of-Check to Time-of-Use (TOCTOU) race condition in how storage quotas were enforced. The application would check a user's current storage usage against their quota, and then, in a separate operation, apply the storage increase. Concurrent requests could bypass the quota check, leading to multiple writes exceeding the limit before the total storage was updated.

### Impact

An attacker could bypass storage quotas, allowing them to store an unlimited amount of data. This could lead to a storage-based denial of service by exhausting available disk space on the server.

### Key diff

```
--- a/inventory/user.go
+++ b/inventory/user.go
@@ -239,14 +252,38 @@ func (c *userClient) ApplyStorageDiff(ctx context.Context, diffs StorageDiff) error {
 
 	return ae.Aggregate()
 }
+
+// ReserveStorage atomically adds size bytes to user uid's storage while
+// enforcing an optional quota. See UserClient.ReserveStorage.
+func (c *userClient) ReserveStorage(ctx context.Context, uid int, size, maxTotal int64) error {
+	// ... (omitted code)
+
+	q := c.client.User.Update().Where(user.ID(uid))
+	if maxTotal > 0 {
+		// storage + size <= maxTotal  <=>  storage <= maxTotal - size.
+		q = q.Where(user.StorageLTE(maxTotal - size))
+	}
+	n, err := q.AddStorage(size).Save(ctx)
+	if err != nil {
+		return err
+	}
+	if n == 0 {
+		return ErrInsufficientCapacity
+	}
+	return nil
+}
+
+// ReleaseStorage unconditionally subtracts size bytes from user uid's
+// storage. Like ReserveStorage, it does not retry on MySQL deadlocks.
+func (c *userClient) ReleaseStorage(ctx context.Context, uid int, size int64) error {
+	// ... (omitted code)
+	return c.client.User.Update().Where(user.ID(uid)).AddStorage(-size).Exec(ctx)
+}
 
 func (c *userClient) CalculateStorage(ctx context.Context, uid int) (int64, error) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `ReserveStorage` method that atomically adds storage and enforces the quota at the database level using a single `UPDATE` statement with a `WHERE` clause. This ensures that the storage increase only proceeds if the new total remains within the quota, preventing race conditions. A corresponding `ReleaseStorage` method is also added for cleanup.

## GHSA-274f-6w77-8qm9 · @sync-in/server

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 6.8 · Pattern: `DOS→RESOURCE_EXHAUSTION`

### Root cause

The application did not correctly increment failed login attempts for Two-Factor Authentication (2FA) when TOTP was enabled. Specifically, the `preservePasswordAttempts` flag was set to true even on failed 2FA attempts, preventing the lockout mechanism from triggering.

### Impact

An attacker could repeatedly guess TOTP codes without being locked out, eventually bypassing the 2FA protection and gaining unauthorized access to user accounts.

### Key diff

```
--- a/backend/src/applications/users/services/users-manager.service.ts
+++ b/backend/src/applications/users/services/users-manager.service.ts
@@ -180,7 +180,7 @@ export class UsersManager {
   }
 
   async updateAccesses(user: UserModel, ip: string, success: boolean, isAuthTwoFa = false) {
-    const preservePasswordAttempts = !isAuthTwoFa && configuration.auth.mfa.totp.enabled && user.twoFaEnabled
+    const preservePasswordAttempts = success && !isAuthTwoFa && configuration.auth.mfa.totp.enabled && user.twoFaEnabled
     if (!(await this.usersQueries.updateAccesses(user.id, ip, preservePasswordAttempts ? 'preserve' : success ? 'reset' : 'increment'))) {
```

### Fix summary

The patch modifies the `preservePasswordAttempts` logic to ensure that failed 2FA attempts (when `success` is false) correctly increment the failed attempt counter, leading to account lockout after a certain number of incorrect guesses.

## GHSA-jx63-h26r-8cph · @sync-in/server

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 6.5 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→REGEX`

### Root cause

The application directly converted user-supplied string input for 'pathFilters' into a regular expression without proper validation or sanitization. This allowed an attacker to provide a 'catastrophic backtracking' regex pattern, which could consume excessive CPU resources during evaluation.

### Impact

An attacker could craft a malicious regular expression that, when processed by the server, would lead to a denial of service by consuming all available CPU resources, making the server unresponsive to legitimate requests.

### Key diff

```
-	@Transform(({ value }) => (typeof value === 'string' && value.length > 0 ? new RegExp(value, 'i') : null))
+	@Transform(({ value }) => transformPathFilters(value))

+export function transformPathFilters(value: unknown): RegExp | null {
+  // ... validation logic including safeRegex check ...
+  if (!safeRegex(pathFilter, { limit: MAX_PATH_FILTER_REPETITIONS })) {
+    throw new BadRequestException('Unsafe path filter pattern')
+  }
+  return pathFilter
+}
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new utility function `transformPathFilters` that validates the user-supplied regex string. It checks for maximum length, ensures it's a valid regex, and critically, uses the `safe-regex2` library to detect and reject patterns prone to catastrophic backtracking, throwing a `BadRequestException` if any of these checks fail.

## GHSA-798p-78g2-v556 · @aborruso/ckan-mcp-server

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.7 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The previous fix for SSRF only blocked direct IP literals that were internal/private. It did not prevent an attacker from providing a hostname that, when resolved via DNS, pointed to an internal IP address. This allowed for Server-Side Request Forgery by bypassing the initial URL validation.

### Impact

An attacker could make the server send requests to internal network resources, potentially accessing sensitive data, interacting with internal services, or scanning the internal network.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/utils/http.ts
+++ b/src/utils/http.ts
@@ -265,38 +302,14 @@ export function validateServerUrl(serverUrl: string): void {
     throw new Error(`Access to "${hostname}" is not allowed.`);
   }
 
-  // Block IPv4 private/special ranges
-  const ipv4 = hostname.match(/^(\d+)\.(\d+)\.(\d+)\.(\d+)$/);
-  if (ipv4) {
-    const [o1, o2] = ipv4.slice(1).map(Number);
-    const blocked = ...;
-    if (blocked) {
-      throw new Error(`Access to private/internal IP addresses is not allowed.`);
-    }
+  // Block IPv4 private/special literals
+  if (/^\d+\.\d+\.\d+\.\d+$/.test(hostname) && isBlockedIp(hostname)) {
+    throw new Error(`Access to private/internal IP addresses is not allowed.`);
   }
 
-  // Block IPv6 private/loopback
-  if (hostname.startsWith('[')) {
-    const ipv6 = hostname.slice(1, -1);
-    const lower = ipv6.toLowerCase();
-    const blockedIpv6 = ...;
-    if (blockedIpv6) {
-      throw new Error(`Access to private/internal IPv6 addresses is not allowed.`);
-    }
+  // Block IPv6 private/loopback literals (URL hostname keeps the brackets)
+  if (hostname.startsWith('[') && isBlockedIp(hostname.slice(1, -1))) {
+    throw new Error(`Access to private/internal IPv6 addresses is not allowed.`);
   }
 
   // Optional domain allowlist: CKAN_ALLOWED_DOMAINS=domain1.com,domain2.org
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `isBlockedIp` function to check for private/internal IP ranges for both IPv4 and IPv6. This function is now used not only for direct IP literals in `validateServerUrl` but also integrated into a new `createSsrfSafeLookup` function. This `createSsrfSafeLookup` function is used to build SSRF-safe HTTP/HTTPS agents that perform DNS resolution and block any resolved IP address that falls within a private/internal range, preventing DNS-based SSRF bypasses.

## GHSA-29hq-23m2-2j47 · @sync-in/server

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `TIMING_SIDE_CHANNEL→USER_ENUM`

### Root cause

The application's login process had a timing side-channel vulnerability. When a user did not exist or was rejected by policy, the password comparison function (bcrypt) was called with a null password hash, which could execute faster than comparing against a real hash. This difference in execution time could be exploited to determine if a username was valid.

### Impact

An attacker could enumerate valid usernames or logins on the system by observing the response times of login attempts. This information could then be used for targeted brute-force attacks or other social engineering tactics.

### Key diff

```
--- a/backend/src/applications/users/services/users-manager.service.ts
+++ b/backend/src/applications/users/services/users-manager.service.ts
@@ -94,12 +94,24 @@
     if (!user) {
       this.logger.warn({ tag: this.validateLocalPasswordForUser.name, msg: `login or email not found for *${loginOrEmail}*` })
       await comparePassword(password, null)
+      if (scope) {
+        // Scoped auth can also check app passwords; burn that second bcrypt path too.
+        await comparePassword(password, null)
+      }
       return null
     }
     if (canAuthenticate && !canAuthenticate(user)) {
       await comparePassword(password, null)
+      if (scope) {
+        // Match the scoped wrong-password path without revealing why local auth was rejected.
+        await comparePassword(password, null)
+      }
       return null
     }
     return this.logUser(user, password, ip, scope)
   }
 
   async logUser(user: UserModel, password: string, ip: string, scope?: AUTH_SCOPE): Promise<UserModel | null> {
     this.validateUserAccess(user, ip)
     let authSuccess: boolean = await comparePassword(password, user.password)
@@ -285,13 +324,24 @@
   }
 
   async validateAppPassword(user: UserModel, password: string, ip: string, scope: AUTH_SCOPE): Promise<boolean> {
-    if (!scope || !user.haveRole(USER_ROLE.USER)) return false
+    if (!scope) return false
+    if (!user.haveRole(USER_ROLE.USER)) {
+      // Keep scoped auth timing close even when app passwords cannot apply to this role.
+      await comparePassword(password, null)
+      return false
+    }
     const secrets = await this.usersQueries.getUserSecrets(user.id)
-    if (!Array.isArray(secrets.appPasswords)) return false
+    if (!Array.isArray(secrets.appPasswords)) {
+      // No candidate app password exists, but the scoped auth path should still do bcrypt work.
+      await comparePassword(password, null)
+      return false
+    }
+    let hasComparedAppPassword = false
     for (const p of secrets.appPasswords) {
       if (p.app !== scope) continue
       const expMs = p.expiration ? new Date(p.expiration) : null
       if (p.expiration && new Date() > expMs) continue // expired
+      hasComparedAppPassword = true
       if (await comparePassword(password, p.password)) {
         p.lastAccess = p.currentAccess
         p.currentAcc
```

### Fix summary

The patch addresses the timing side-channel by ensuring that the password comparison function (comparePassword) is always called, even when the user does not exist or is rejected by policy. It passes 'null' as the stored hash in these cases, forcing the bcrypt operation to take a consistent amount of time, thus eliminating the timing difference.

## GHSA-32gc-64m7-hj7v · 9router

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **MODERATE** 5.3 · Pattern: `UNCLASSIFIED`

### Root cause

The vulnerability description mentions a 'Spoofable X-9r-Real-Ip Header' leading to a login brute-force lockout bypass. However, the provided diff does not contain any changes related to IP header processing or brute-force lockout logic. Instead, the diff shows changes to an OAuth provider configuration, specifically for 'CodeBuddy' (renamed to 'codebuddy-cn'). The changes involve modifying the HTTP method for token polling from POST to GET, adjusting headers, and adding a new token refresh function. This suggests the provided diff does not directly address the described vulnerability but rather refactors or corrects an OAuth integration.

### Impact

Based on the advisory summary, an attacker could bypass login brute-force lockouts, potentially enabling unlimited attempts to guess credentials for user accounts. The provided diff, however, does not show changes that would directly mitigate this specific impact.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/lib/oauth/providers.js
+++ b/src/lib/oauth/providers.js
-      const response = await fetch(config.tokenUrl, {
-        method: "POST",
+      const response = await fetch(`${config.tokenUrl}?state=${encodeURIComponent(deviceCode)}`, {
+        method: "GET",
         headers: {
-          "Content-Type": "application/json",
           Accept: "application/json",
```

### Fix summary

The patch renames the 'codebuddy' OAuth provider to 'codebuddy-cn', changes the token polling mechanism from a POST request with a JSON body to a GET request with the state as a query parameter, and adds several new headers. It also introduces a new `refreshCodebuddyToken` function for handling token refreshes using a specific header for the refresh token.

## GHSA-34fc-gh42-pj53 · github.com/openbao/openbao

2026-09-22 · Go · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `TIMING_SIDE_CHANNEL→TOKEN_LEAK`

### Root cause

The vulnerability existed because the comparison of the provided recovery token with the actual token was done using a standard string comparison operator (`!=`). This operator can short-circuit, meaning it stops comparing characters as soon as a mismatch is found, leading to varying execution times depending on the position of the first differing character.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially perform a timing attack to deduce the recovery token character by character. This could lead to unauthorized access to the recovery mode, allowing them to perform sensitive operations.

### Key diff

```
-		if reqToken == "" || token.Load() == "" || reqToken != token.Load() {
+		rToken, rTokenOk := token.Load().(string)
+		if len(reqToken) == 0 || !rTokenOk || len(rToken) == 0 || subtle.ConstantTimeCompare([]byte(reqToken), []byte(rToken)) == 0 {
```

### Fix summary

The patch replaces the standard string comparison with `subtle.ConstantTimeCompare`. This function ensures that the comparison takes a constant amount of time, regardless of the input strings, thereby preventing timing side-channel attacks.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 7 occurrences**
> Languages: JavaScript, PHP, Java, Python, Go
> → GHSA-jj6q-rrrf-h66h
> → GHSA-r854-jrxh-36qx
> → GHSA-xmj9-7625-f634
> → GHSA-7r92-3jgr-r65q
> → GHSA-g6cj-pr64-35w5
> → GHSA-8jqh-95g6-7jpj

## GHSA-59w7-v8rr-pr4p · github.com/openbao/openbao

2026-09-22 · Go · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→TEMPLATE`

### Root cause

The vulnerability stemmed from insufficient sanitization of templated policy values, specifically in the `default_user` and `allowed_principals` fields. When templating was enabled, an attacker could inject wildcard characters (like commas) into the rendered principal names, bypassing intended restrictions and potentially gaining unauthorized access or control over SSH certificates.

### Impact

An attacker could craft malicious input to the templated policies, leading to the issuance of SSH certificates with arbitrary principal names. This could allow them to impersonate other users or services, effectively escalating privileges within the system.

### Key diff

```
--- a/builtin/logical/ssh/path_issue_sign.go
+++ b/builtin/logical/ssh/path_issue_sign.go
@@ -79,7 +80,7 @@ func (b *backend) pathSignIssueCertificateHelper(sc *storageContext, req *logica
 	} else {
 		defaultPrincipal := role.DefaultUser
 		if role.DefaultUserTemplate {
-			defaultPrincipal, err = b.renderPrincipal(role.DefaultUser, req)
+			defaultPrincipal, err = b.renderPrincipal(role.DefaultUser, req, true)
 			if err != nil
 				return nil, err
 			}
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces a new `AllowCommasInSubstitutions` flag for SSH roles. By default, templated substitutions for principals will now block commas, preventing injection. The `renderPrincipal` function was updated to use a more robust identity templating helper (`identitytpl.PopulateString`) which allows for explicit blocking of specific characters in substitutions.

> ⚡ **Pattern match — 26 occurrences**
> Languages: Java, PHP, Python, JavaScript, Go

---

## GHSA-f6pj-qv47-g96w · mcp-atlassian

2026-09-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application was vulnerable to Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF) due to a lack of redirect validation and DNS rebinding protection, allowing attackers to access internal resources. Additionally, the JQL project filtering logic was insufficient, potentially allowing JQL injection to bypass project restrictions if an attacker crafted specific JQL queries.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially access internal network resources, bypass configured project restrictions in Jira, and exfiltrate sensitive data or perform unauthorized actions within the Jira instance.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
+++ b/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
@@ -130,8 +132,18 @@ def __init__(self, config: ConfluenceConfig | None = None) -> None:
             client_key_password=self.config.client_key_password,
         )
 
-        # Apply opt-in HTTP hardening after SSL setup so SSLIgnoreAdapter, if
-        # mounted, also picks up the configured behavior.
+        # Validate redirects for SSRF on every outbound call from this session
+        # (covers direct _session.get() paths and global/stdio fetchers, not just
+        # the per-user HTTP path).
+        self.confluence._session.hooks["response"].append(make_ssrf_redirect_hook())
+        # Pin DNS resolution against rebinding: resolve+validate once and connect
+        # to that address, closing the validate→reconnect TOCTOU. Preserves TLS SNI.
+        mount_ssrf_pinning(self.confluence._session)
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces SSRF protection by adding a redirect validation hook and mounting an SSRF pinning adapter to prevent DNS rebinding. For Jira, it refactors the JQL project filtering to ensure that the configured project allowlist is always applied and properly ANDed with user-supplied JQL, preventing bypasses.

## GHSA-jjhp-8crj-mppq · @roomi-fields/notebooklm-mcp

2026-09-22 · JavaScript · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `PATH_TRAVERSAL→FILE_WRITE`

### Root cause

The application allowed user-supplied input for the `vault_dir` parameter to be directly used in `path.resolve`, which could lead to directory traversal. Additionally, the `prefix` used in `makeSlug` was not sanitized, potentially allowing path separators in generated filenames.

### Impact

An attacker could write arbitrary files to any location on the file system accessible by the application, outside of the intended vault directory.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/utils/vault-writer.ts
+++ b/src/utils/vault-writer.ts
@@ -259,7 +302,7 @@ export async function runBatchToVault(
     session_id,
   } = args;
 
-  const absVaultDir = path.resolve(vault_dir);
+  const absVaultDir = resolveVaultDir(vault_dir);
   await fs.mkdir(absVaultDir, { recursive: true });
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces `resolveVaultDir` to validate and confine the `vault_dir` to a specified root, if `NOTEBOOKLM_VAULT_ROOT` is set. It also adds `sanitizeSlugPrefix` to strip path separators and traversal sequences from the `prefix` used in slug generation, preventing path traversal in filenames.

## GHSA-mrq8-fv7v-hhjg · mcp-atlassian

2026-09-22 · Python · **HIGH** 0.0 · Pattern: `SSRF→INTERNAL_ACCESS`

### Root cause

The application allowed HTTP upload tools to accept arbitrary server-local file paths, leading to Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF). Additionally, the JQL query construction for Jira search did not properly enforce project filtering, allowing a user-supplied 'projects_filter' to potentially bypass or broaden the configured project allowlist, leading to JQL injection.

### Impact

An attacker could potentially force the server to make requests to internal resources or arbitrary external URLs (SSRF). In the Jira context, an attacker could craft JQL queries to access data from projects they are not authorized for, bypassing configured project restrictions.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
+++ b/src/mcp_atlassian/confluence/client.py
@@ -130,8 +132,18 @@ def __init__(self, config: ConfluenceConfig | None = None) -> None:
             client_key_password=self.config.client_key_password,
         )
 
-        # Apply opt-in HTTP hardening after SSL setup so SSLIgnoreAdapter, if
-        # mounted, also picks up the configured behavior.
+        # Validate redirects for SSRF on every outbound call from this session
+        # (covers direct _session.get() paths and global/stdio fetchers, not just
+        # the per-user HTTP path).
+        self.confluence._session.hooks["response"].append(make_ssrf_redirect_hook())
+        # Pin DNS resolution against rebinding: resolve+validate once and connect
+        # to that address, closing the validate→reconnect TOCTOU. Preserves TLS SNI.
+        mount_ssrf_pinning(self.confluence._session)
+
+        # Apply opt-in HTTP hardening after SSL setup and after the pinning
+        # adapter is mounted: these wrappers patch send() in place on whatever
+        # adapters are mounted now, so mounting the pinning adapter later would
+        # silently drop them.
         configure_retry(self.confluence._session, service="Confluence")
         configure_concurrency(self.confluence._session, service="Confluence")
         configure_rate_limit(self.confluence._session, service="Confluence")
```

### Fix summary

The patch introduces SSRF protection by adding a redirect hook and DNS pinning to the HTTP session. For Jira, it refactors the project filtering logic to ensure that the configured 'projects_filter' is always applied as a hard boundary, and any user-supplied filter can only narrow results within this boundary, preventing JQL injection for project filtering.

## GHSA-wx4m-69m9-gx3m · homeassistant

2026-09-22 · Python · **CRITICAL** 0.0 · Pattern: `UNSANITIZED_INPUT→XSS`

### Root cause

The application was vulnerable to Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) because it directly used user-controlled input, specifically the 'name' or 'id' property of series data, within the ECharts configuration without proper sanitization. While a global XSS filter was present, it was not applied to these specific fields, allowing malicious scripts to be injected and executed in the browser.

### Impact

An attacker could inject arbitrary JavaScript code into the statistics graph card. This could lead to session hijacking, defacement of the user interface, or redirection to malicious sites, compromising the integrity and confidentiality of the user's interaction with Home Assistant.

### Key diff

```
--- a/src/components/chart/ha-chart-base.ts
+++ b/src/components/chart/ha-chart-base.ts
-import { filterXSS } from "../../common/util/xss";
+import { wrapLitTooltipFormatter } from "./lit-tooltip-formatter";
...
-      const name = filterXSS(String(s.name ?? s.id ?? ""));
-      return { ...s, name, data };
+      return processSeriesTooltipFormatter(result);
```

### Fix summary

The patch removes the direct `filterXSS` call on the series name and instead introduces a new `wrapLitTooltipFormatter` function. This function ensures that any user-provided formatter functions for tooltips are properly wrapped to prevent XSS, and it also processes series data to ensure tooltips are handled securely. Additionally, it ensures that tooltip options are shallow-copied to prevent unintended mutations.
